Yeah… a lot, actually. I used to like doing these little things that weren’t anything big, but they felt like mine, you know? Like I’d go walk by the lake, just kind of clear my head and watch the water, take my time out there. I used to really enjoy that.

And I had these little model kits I’d work on at home. Nothing fancy, just sitting there with the pieces, putting things together bit by bit. It used to feel calming, like I could get absorbed in it.

Now… they just sit there. The boxes and pieces are still there, and I’ll look at them, but it’s like they belong to somebody else. I don’t feel that pull to pick them up. Even walking by the lake sounds like something I should want to do, but I don’t really feel anything about it. It’s like the enjoyment part got switched off somehow.

I… I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I don’t want to say the wrong thing and make it sound bigger or smaller than it is.

I guess… I’ve had moments where I feel really worn out by everything, like I don’t want to deal with the day, but when you say “life isn’t worth living” or “hurting myself,” I don’t know if I’d put it that way. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… no, it’s not really like that. I’m not avoiding food because I’m scared of gaining weight or anything. I don’t think about it that way.

It’s more like… I just hardly eat right anymore. Food doesn’t really pull me in. Some days I’ll get through most of the day on coffee, and then maybe I’ll have a piece of bread or something small, just because it’s there or because I realize I haven’t had anything. It’s not me trying to lose weight.

I’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.

Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. I got fired, and after that everything sort of… swung really hard. At first it wasn’t just sadness right away, it was like I got this scary kind of driven feeling, like I had to do something, had to prove something, had to keep moving. Yeah… yeah, I was. That part was really strange, because normally if I only sleep a little, I feel awful the next day. But during that period, I’d sleep maybe two or three hours, sometimes barely that, and then I’d just kind of pop up like I’d had a full night’s rest.

It’s not me trying to lose weight.\n\nI’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a particular stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I did. I mean, “euphoric” sounds a little strong, but I guess there were times it was like that. Right after I got fired, instead of just feeling crushed, I got this feeling like… fine, I’ll show them. Like I was fired up, almost untouchable. Like nobody could really stop me or tell me what to do.

It wasn’t just confidence like a normal good mood, though. It felt bigger than that, like I was suddenly sure I had everything figured out. And if anyone tried to bring me down or say, “Maybe slow down,” or “Think this through,” I’d get irritated really fast. I’d snap at them before they even finished talking, because in my head they were just getting in the way.

So yeah, there was that kind of high, charged-up confidence… but it had an angry edge to it too.

I just know I’ve been feeling really low, and it’s been hard to care about much.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food because you’re worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No… no, it’s not really like that. I’m not avoiding food because I’m scared of gaining weight or anything. I don’t think about it that way.\n\nIt’s more like… I just hardly eat right anymore. Food doesn’t really pull me in. Some days I’ll get through most of the day on coffee, and then maybe I’ll have a piece of bread or something small, just because it’s there or because I realize I haven’t had anything. It’s not me trying to lose weight.\n\nI’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a particular stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. I got fired, and after that everything sort of… swung really hard. At first it wasn’t just sadness right away, it was like I got this scary kind of driven feeling, like I had to do something, had to prove something, had to keep moving. It felt intense, not normal for me.\n\nAnd then after that, I dropped down into this low place. That whole swing — from getting fired, to feeling all pushed and wound up, and then ending up like this — has been going on about a month now. It feels longer when I’m in it, honestly, but if I count it out, yeah… around a month.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period after you got fired, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I was. That part was really strange, because normally if I only sleep a little, I feel awful the next day. But during that period, I’d sleep maybe two or three hours, sometimes barely that, and then I’d just kind of pop up like I’d had a full night’s rest.\n\nIt wasn’t even like I was dragging myself out of bed. I’d wake up and feel like, “Okay, I’m up, I’ve got things to do,” like my body didn’t understand it was supposed to be tired. I remember looking at the time and thinking it was ridiculous, because it would still be dark out, and I should’ve been exhausted, but I wasn’t. I was just… going.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I mean, “euphoric” sounds a little strong, but I guess there were times it was like that. Right after I got fired, instead of just feeling crushed, I got this feeling like… fine, I’ll show them. Like I was fired up, almost untouchable. Like nobody could really stop me or tell me what to do.\n\nIt wasn’t just confidence like a normal good mood, though. It felt bigger than that, like I was suddenly sure I had everything figured out. And if anyone tried to bring me down or say, “Maybe slow down,” or “Think this through,” I’d get irritated really fast. I’d snap at them before they even finished talking, because in my head they were just getting in the way.\n\nSo yeah, there was that kind of high, charged-up confidence… but it had an angry edge to it too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, definitely. I was doing way more than I normally do. It was like I couldn’t sit still at all, like if I sat down for even a minute my body would start buzzing and I’d think, no, I need to get up, I need to do something.

I kept going out and cleaning my tools, even stuff that didn’t really need cleaning. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. And I was moving boxes around too — not even always to a better place, just shifting things, sorting, re-sorting, deciding I had a new plan, then changing it again.

And I kept planning jobs in my head, like all these things I was going to do, ways to make money or fix things or get back on track. It felt like I had a motor running inside me. Not just being productive, more like I couldn’t turn it off.

The boxes and pieces are still there, and I’ll look at them, but it’s like they belong to somebody else. I don’t feel that pull to pick them up. Even walking by the lake sounds like something I should want to do, but I don’t really feel anything about it. It’s like the enjoyment part got switched off somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I don’t want to say the wrong thing and make it sound bigger or smaller than it is.\n\nI guess… I’ve had moments where I feel really worn out by everything, like I don’t want to deal with the day, but when you say “life isn’t worth living” or “hurting myself,” I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I haven’t really sat down and thought it through like that, if that makes sense. It’s more like this tired, empty feeling, and I just kind of go quiet with it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed myself making any kind of plan or anything like that. I just know I’ve been feeling really low, and it’s been hard to care about much.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food because you’re worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No… no, it’s not really like that. I’m not avoiding food because I’m scared of gaining weight or anything. I don’t think about it that way.\n\nIt’s more like… I just hardly eat right anymore. Food doesn’t really pull me in. Some days I’ll get through most of the day on coffee, and then maybe I’ll have a piece of bread or something small, just because it’s there or because I realize I haven’t had anything. It’s not me trying to lose weight.\n\nI’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a particular stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. I got fired, and after that everything sort of… swung really hard. At first it wasn’t just sadness right away, it was like I got this scary kind of driven feeling, like I had to do something, had to prove something, had to keep moving. It felt intense, not normal for me.\n\nAnd then after that, I dropped down into this low place. That whole swing — from getting fired, to feeling all pushed and wound up, and then ending up like this — has been going on about a month now. It feels longer when I’m in it, honestly, but if I count it out, yeah… around a month.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period after you got fired, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I was. That part was really strange, because normally if I only sleep a little, I feel awful the next day. But during that period, I’d sleep maybe two or three hours, sometimes barely that, and then I’d just kind of pop up like I’d had a full night’s rest.\n\nIt wasn’t even like I was dragging myself out of bed. I’d wake up and feel like, “Okay, I’m up, I’ve got things to do,” like my body didn’t understand it was supposed to be tired. I remember looking at the time and thinking it was ridiculous, because it would still be dark out, and I should’ve been exhausted, but I wasn’t. I was just… going.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I mean, “euphoric” sounds a little strong, but I guess there were times it was like that. Right after I got fired, instead of just feeling crushed, I got this feeling like… fine, I’ll show them. Like I was fired up, almost untouchable. Like nobody could really stop me or tell me what to do.\n\nIt wasn’t just confidence like a normal good mood, though. It felt bigger than that, like I was suddenly sure I had everything figured out. And if anyone tried to bring me down or say, “Maybe slow down,” or “Think this through,” I’d get irritated really fast. I’d snap at them before they even finished talking, because in my head they were just getting in the way.\n\nSo yeah, there was that kind of high, charged-up confidence… but it had an angry edge to it too.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I was doing way more than I normally do. It was like I couldn’t sit still at all, like if I sat down for even a minute my body would start buzzing and I’d think, no, I need to get up, I need to do something.\n\nI kept going out and cleaning my tools, even stuff that didn’t really need cleaning. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. And I was moving boxes around too — not even always to a better place, just shifting things, sorting, re-sorting, deciding I had a new plan, then changing it again.\n\nAnd I kept planning jobs in my head, like all these things I was going to do, ways to make money or fix things or get back on track. It felt like I had a motor running inside me. Not just being productive, more like I couldn’t turn it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… I guess I was. I didn’t really notice it at first, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining what needed to happen, you know? Like I had all these thoughts lined up and I had to get them out before I lost them.

But my sister pointed it out. She said I was talking so fast she could barely get a word in. I was going on and on about prices, and making flyers, and what to say to tenants, and how much things should cost, and how I was going to handle everything. I remember her trying to ask me something, and I’d just keep talking over her, not even meaning to be rude exactly, but I couldn’t stop long enough to let her catch up.

It was like my mouth was trying to keep up with my head, and there was too much coming at once.

It’s just this low, dark kind of feeling sitting on me most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot, actually. I used to like doing these little things that weren’t anything big, but they felt like mine, you know? Nothing fancy, just sitting there with the pieces, putting things together bit by bit. It used to feel calming, like I could get absorbed in it.\n\nNow… they just sit there. The boxes and pieces are still there, and I’ll look at them, but it’s like they belong to somebody else. I don’t feel that pull to pick them up. It’s like the enjoyment part got switched off somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I don’t want to say the wrong thing and make it sound bigger or smaller than it is.\n\nI guess… I’ve had moments where I feel really worn out by everything, like I don’t want to deal with the day, but when you say “life isn’t worth living” or “hurting myself,” I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Some days I’ll get through most of the day on coffee, and then maybe I’ll have a piece of bread or something small, just because it’s there or because I realize I haven’t had anything. It’s not me trying to lose weight.\n\nI’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a particular stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. I got fired, and after that everything sort of… swung really hard. At first it wasn’t just sadness right away, it was like I got this scary kind of driven feeling, like I had to do something, had to prove something, had to keep moving. It felt intense, not normal for me.\n\nAnd then after that, I dropped down into this low place. That whole swing — from getting fired, to feeling all pushed and wound up, and then ending up like this — has been going on about a month now. It feels longer when I’m in it, honestly, but if I count it out, yeah… around a month.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period after you got fired, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I was. That part was really strange, because normally if I only sleep a little, I feel awful the next day. I’d wake up and feel like, “Okay, I’m up, I’ve got things to do,” like my body didn’t understand it was supposed to be tired. I was just… going.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It felt bigger than that, like I was suddenly sure I had everything figured out. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. I’d snap at them before they even finished talking, because in my head they were just getting in the way.\n\nSo yeah, there was that kind of high, charged-up confidence… but it had an angry edge to it too.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I was doing way more than I normally do. It was like I couldn’t sit still at all, like if I sat down for even a minute my body would start buzzing and I’d think, no, I need to get up, I need to do something.\n\nI kept going out and cleaning my tools, even stuff that didn’t really need cleaning. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. And I was moving boxes around too — not even always to a better place, just shifting things, sorting, re-sorting, deciding I had a new plan, then changing it again.\n\nAnd I kept planning jobs in my head, like all these things I was going to do, ways to make money or fix things or get back on track. It felt like I had a motor running inside me. Not just being productive, more like I couldn’t turn it off.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you talking a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was. I didn’t really notice it at first, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining what needed to happen, you know? Like I had all these thoughts lined up and I had to get them out before I lost them.\n\nBut my sister pointed it out. She said I was talking so fast she could barely get a word in. I was going on and on about prices, and making flyers, and what to say to tenants, and how much things should cost, and how I was going to handle everything. I remember her trying to ask me something, and I’d just keep talking over her, not even meaning to be rude exactly, but I couldn’t stop long enough to let her catch up.\n\nIt was like my mouth was trying to keep up with my head, and there was too much coming at once.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that charged-up period, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, “risky” can mean different things, right? Like, if you mean did I go out and do something crazy dangerous on purpose, I don’t know that I’d say it that way.

During that time I was definitely acting faster than I normally would, like making decisions before really thinking them through all the way. But when you ask if it was unusually risky or impulsive, I kind of have to stop and think, because in the moment it didn’t feel risky to me. It felt like I was just doing what needed to be done, like I was finally moving instead of sitting around.

Other people might’ve seen it as me being impulsive, I guess, because I wasn’t exactly pausing and weighing things out. But I don’t have one clear thing in my head where I can say, yes, that was definitely some big risky thing. It was more like everything felt urgent, and I’d just jump into stuff without the usual hesitation.

I mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I don’t want to say the wrong thing and make it sound bigger or smaller than it is.\n\nI guess… I’ve had moments where I feel really worn out by everything, like I don’t want to deal with the day, but when you say “life isn’t worth living” or “hurting myself,” I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I haven’t really sat down and thought it through like that, if that makes sense. It’s more like this tired, empty feeling, and I just kind of go quiet with it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed myself making any kind of plan or anything like that. talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My sister said I was talking so fast about prices, flyers, and tenants that she could barely get a word in.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My head was full of ideas all at once, one job turning into another plan, and I could not slow it down even when I tried.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started calling old tenants, making lists, pricing tools, and trying to line up handyman work all day, way more than I ever did before.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: Since this got bad, I have needed my relatives to help with money, rides, and paperwork, and I am not keeping up with basic errands the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The scary swing from feeling driven after I was fired to being this low has been going on for about a month.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 5 months\n  manifestation: This depressed spell has been hanging over me for about five months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I was doing way more than I normally do. It was like I couldn’t sit still at all, like if I sat down for even a minute my body would start buzzing and I’d think, no, I need to get up, I need to do something.\n\nI kept going out and cleaning my tools, even stuff that didn’t really need cleaning. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. And I was moving boxes around too — not even always to a better place, just shifting things, sorting, re-sorting, deciding I had a new plan, then changing it again.\n\nAnd I kept planning jobs in my head, like all these things I was going to do, ways to make money or fix things or get back on track. It felt like I had a motor running inside me. Not just being productive, more like I couldn’t turn it off.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you talking a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was. I didn’t really notice it at first, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining what needed to happen, you know? Like I had all these thoughts lined up and I had to get them out before I lost them.\n\nBut my sister pointed it out. She said I was talking so fast she could barely get a word in. I was going on and on about prices, and making flyers, and what to say to tenants, and how much things should cost, and how I was going to handle everything. I remember her trying to ask me something, and I’d just keep talking over her, not even meaning to be rude exactly, but I couldn’t stop long enough to let her catch up.\n\nIt was like my mouth was trying to keep up with my head, and there was too much coming at once.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, “risky” can mean different things, right? Like, if you mean did I go out and do something crazy dangerous on purpose, I don’t know that I’d say it that way.\n\nDuring that time I was definitely acting faster than I normally would, like making decisions before really thinking them through all the way. But when you ask if it was unusually risky or impulsive, I kind of have to stop and think, because in the moment it didn’t feel risky to me. It felt like I was just doing what needed to be done, like I was finally moving instead of sitting around.\n\nOther people might’ve seen it as me being impulsive, I guess, because I wasn’t exactly pausing and weighing things out. But I don’t have one clear thing in my head where I can say, yes, that was definitely some big risky thing. It was more like everything felt urgent, and I’d just jump into stuff without the usual hesitation.\n\n[Last Question]\nAbout how many days in a row did that charged-up period last?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

It’s kind of hard to pin down exactly to the day, because it didn’t feel like it started and stopped clean, like flipping a switch. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.81, F32.A, F41.9, F50.9, F54\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the biggest thing is I’m not really eating right anymore. Like, I’ll go most of the day and it’s just coffee, maybe a piece of bread if I can make myself grab something. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or anything, it just… food doesn’t really happen. And now my pants are getting loose, like hanging on me, and that kind of scared me when I noticed it. I figured I should probably talk to somebody because it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. Most days it’s like I wake up and before I even really think about anything, there’s this heavy feeling there. Like right in my chest, kind of empty and weighed down at the same time, if that makes sense. It’s not even always that I’m crying or anything dramatic, it’s more like… I open my eyes and it already feels like nothing good is waiting for me. Like the day is just something I have to get through.\n\nI find myself wanting to keep my head down, not really look too far ahead, not think too much. It’s just this low, dark kind of feeling sitting on me most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot, actually. I used to like doing these little things that weren’t anything big, but they felt like mine, you know? Like I’d go walk by the lake, just kind of clear my head and watch the water, take my time out there. I used to really enjoy that.\n\nAnd I had these little model kits I’d work on at home. Nothing fancy, just sitting there with the pieces, putting things together bit by bit. It used to feel calming, like I could get absorbed in it.\n\nNow… they just sit there. The boxes and pieces are still there, and I’ll look at them, but it’s like they belong to somebody else. I don’t feel that pull to pick them up. Even walking by the lake sounds like something I should want to do, but I don’t really feel anything about it. It’s like the enjoyment part got switched off somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I don’t want to say the wrong thing and make it sound bigger or smaller than it is.\n\nI guess… I’ve had moments where I feel really worn out by everything, like I don’t want to deal with the day, but when you say “life isn’t worth living” or “hurting myself,” I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I haven’t really sat down and thought it through like that, if that makes sense. It’s more like this tired, empty feeling, and I just kind of go quiet with it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed myself making any kind of plan or anything like that. I just know I’ve been feeling really low, and it’s been hard to care about much.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food because you’re worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No… no, it’s not really like that. I’m not avoiding food because I’m scared of gaining weight or anything. I don’t think about it that way.\n\nIt’s more like… I just hardly eat right anymore. Food doesn’t really pull me in. Some days I’ll get through most of the day on coffee, and then maybe I’ll have a piece of bread or something small, just because it’s there or because I realize I haven’t had anything. It’s not me trying to lose weight.\n\nI’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a particular stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. I got fired, and after that everything sort of… swung really hard. At first it wasn’t just sadness right away, it was like I got this scary kind of driven feeling, like I had to do something, had to prove something, had to keep moving. It felt intense, not normal for me.\n\nAnd then after that, I dropped down into this low place. That whole swing — from getting fired, to feeling all pushed and wound up, and then ending up like this — has been going on about a month now. It feels longer when I’m in it, honestly, but if I count it out, yeah… around a month.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period after you got fired, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I was. That part was really strange, because normally if I only sleep a little, I feel awful the next day. But during that period, I’d sleep maybe two or three hours, sometimes barely that, and then I’d just kind of pop up like I’d had a full night’s rest.\n\nIt wasn’t even like I was dragging myself out of bed. I’d wake up and feel like, “Okay, I’m up, I’ve got things to do,” like my body didn’t understand it was supposed to be tired. I remember looking at the time and thinking it was ridiculous, because it would still be dark out, and I should’ve been exhausted, but I wasn’t. I was just… going.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I mean, “euphoric” sounds a little strong, but I guess there were times it was like that. Right after I got fired, instead of just feeling crushed, I got this feeling like… fine, I’ll show them. Like I was fired up, almost untouchable. Like nobody could really stop me or tell me what to do.\n\nIt wasn’t just confidence like a normal good mood, though. It felt bigger than that, like I was suddenly sure I had everything figured out. And if anyone tried to bring me down or say, “Maybe slow down,” or “Think this through,” I’d get irritated really fast. I’d snap at them before they even finished talking, because in my head they were just getting in the way.\n\nSo yeah, there was that kind of high, charged-up confidence… but it had an angry edge to it too.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I was doing way more than I normally do. It was like I couldn’t sit still at all, like if I sat down for even a minute my body would start buzzing and I’d think, no, I need to get up, I need to do something.\n\nI kept going out and cleaning my tools, even stuff that didn’t really need cleaning. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. And I was moving boxes around too — not even always to a better place, just shifting things, sorting, re-sorting, deciding I had a new plan, then changing it again.\n\nAnd I kept planning jobs in my head, like all these things I was going to do, ways to make money or fix things or get back on track. It felt like I had a motor running inside me. Not just being productive, more like I couldn’t turn it off.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you talking a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was. I didn’t really notice it at first, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining what needed to happen, you know? Like I had all these thoughts lined up and I had to get them out before I lost them.\n\nBut my sister pointed it out. She said I was talking so fast she could barely get a word in. I was going on and on about prices, and making flyers, and what to say to tenants, and how much things should cost, and how I was going to handle everything. I remember her trying to ask me something, and I’d just keep talking over her, not even meaning to be rude exactly, but I couldn’t stop long enough to let her catch up.\n\nIt was like my mouth was trying to keep up with my head, and there was too much coming at once.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, “risky” can mean different things, right? Like, if you mean did I go out and do something crazy dangerous on purpose, I don’t know that I’d say it that way.\n\nDuring that time I was definitely acting faster than I normally would, like making decisions before really thinking them through all the way. But when you ask if it was unusually risky or impulsive, I kind of have to stop and think, because in the moment it didn’t feel risky to me. It felt like I was just doing what needed to be done, like I was finally moving instead of sitting around.\n\nOther people might’ve seen it as me being impulsive, I guess, because I wasn’t exactly pausing and weighing things out. But I don’t have one clear thing in my head where I can say, yes, that was definitely some big risky thing. It was more like everything felt urgent, and I’d just jump into stuff without the usual hesitation.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of hard to pin down exactly to the day, because it didn’t feel like it started and stopped clean, like flipping a switch. But if I’m being honest, that whole scary swing — from when I got fired and then started feeling all driven and wound up, to now being this low — it’s been about a month altogether.\n\nThe charged-up part was in there first, after I got fired, and then it… shifted. But the whole thing, the whole run of it where I haven’t felt like myself, has been around a month.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. I didn’t go to the hospital, no. But serious problems… yeah, I mean, it’s been serious in the sense that I’m not functioning like I normally would.

Since it got bad, I’ve had to lean on my relatives for stuff I used to handle myself. Like money things, getting rides places, dealing with paperwork — forms and calls and things like that. Like I’d go walk by the lake, just kind of clear my head and watch the water, take my time out there. I used to really enjoy that.\n\nAnd I had these little model kits I’d work on at home. Nothing fancy, just sitting there with the pieces, putting things together bit by bit. It used to feel calming, like I could get absorbed in it.\n\nNow… they just sit there. The boxes and pieces are still there, and I’ll look at them, but it’s like they belong to somebody else. I don’t feel that pull to pick them up. Even walking by the lake sounds like something I should want to do, but I don’t really feel anything about it. It’s like the enjoyment part got switched off somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I don’t want to say the wrong thing and make it sound bigger or smaller than it is.\n\nI guess… I’ve had moments where I feel really worn out by everything, like I don’t want to deal with the day, but when you say “life isn’t worth living” or “hurting myself,” I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I haven’t really sat down and thought it through like that, if that makes sense. It’s more like this tired, empty feeling, and I just kind of go quiet with it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed myself making any kind of plan or anything like that. I just know I’ve been feeling really low, and it’s been hard to care about much.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food because you’re worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No… no, it’s not really like that. I’m not avoiding food because I’m scared of gaining weight or anything. I don’t think about it that way.\n\nIt’s more like… I just hardly eat right anymore. Food doesn’t really pull me in. Some days I’ll get through most of the day on coffee, and then maybe I’ll have a piece of bread or something small, just because it’s there or because I realize I haven’t had anything. It’s not me trying to lose weight.\n\nI’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a particular stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. I got fired, and after that everything sort of… swung really hard. At first it wasn’t just sadness right away, it was like I got this scary kind of driven feeling, like I had to do something, had to prove something, had to keep moving. It felt intense, not normal for me.\n\nAnd then after that, I dropped down into this low place. That whole swing — from getting fired, to feeling all pushed and wound up, and then ending up like this — has been going on about a month now. It feels longer when I’m in it, honestly, but if I count it out, yeah… around a month.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period after you got fired, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I was. That part was really strange, because normally if I only sleep a little, I feel awful the next day. But during that period, I’d sleep maybe two or three hours, sometimes barely that, and then I’d just kind of pop up like I’d had a full night’s rest.\n\nIt wasn’t even like I was dragging myself out of bed. I’d wake up and feel like, “Okay, I’m up, I’ve got things to do,” like my body didn’t understand it was supposed to be tired. I remember looking at the time and thinking it was ridiculous, because it would still be dark out, and I should’ve been exhausted, but I wasn’t. I was just… going.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I mean, “euphoric” sounds a little strong, but I guess there were times it was like that. Right after I got fired, instead of just feeling crushed, I got this feeling like… fine, I’ll show them. Like I was fired up, almost untouchable. Like nobody could really stop me or tell me what to do.\n\nIt wasn’t just confidence like a normal good mood, though. It felt bigger than that, like I was suddenly sure I had everything figured out. And if anyone tried to bring me down or say, “Maybe slow down,” or “Think this through,” I’d get irritated really fast. I’d snap at them before they even finished talking, because in my head they were just getting in the way.\n\nSo yeah, there was that kind of high, charged-up confidence… but it had an angry edge to it too.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I was doing way more than I normally do. It was like I couldn’t sit still at all, like if I sat down for even a minute my body would start buzzing and I’d think, no, I need to get up, I need to do something.\n\nI kept going out and cleaning my tools, even stuff that didn’t really need cleaning. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. And I was moving boxes around too — not even always to a better place, just shifting things, sorting, re-sorting, deciding I had a new plan, then changing it again.\n\nAnd I kept planning jobs in my head, like all these things I was going to do, ways to make money or fix things or get back on track. It felt like I had a motor running inside me. Not just being productive, more like I couldn’t turn it off.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you talking a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was. I didn’t really notice it at first, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining what needed to happen, you know? Like I had all these thoughts lined up and I had to get them out before I lost them.\n\nBut my sister pointed it out. She said I was talking so fast she could barely get a word in. I was going on and on about prices, and making flyers, and what to say to tenants, and how much things should cost, and how I was going to handle everything. I remember her trying to ask me something, and I’d just keep talking over her, not even meaning to be rude exactly, but I couldn’t stop long enough to let her catch up.\n\nIt was like my mouth was trying to keep up with my head, and there was too much coming at once.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, “risky” can mean different things, right? Like, if you mean did I go out and do something crazy dangerous on purpose, I don’t know that I’d say it that way.\n\nDuring that time I was definitely acting faster than I normally would, like making decisions before really thinking them through all the way. But when you ask if it was unusually risky or impulsive, I kind of have to stop and think, because in the moment it didn’t feel risky to me. It felt like I was just doing what needed to be done, like I was finally moving instead of sitting around.\n\nOther people might’ve seen it as me being impulsive, I guess, because I wasn’t exactly pausing and weighing things out. But I don’t have one clear thing in my head where I can say, yes, that was definitely some big risky thing. It was more like everything felt urgent, and I’d just jump into stuff without the usual hesitation.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of hard to pin down exactly to the day, because it didn’t feel like it started and stopped clean, like flipping a switch. But if I’m being honest, that whole scary swing — from when I got fired and then started feeling all driven and wound up, to now being this low — it’s been about a month altogether.\n\nThe charged-up part was in there first, after I got fired, and then it… shifted. But the whole thing, the whole run of it where I haven’t felt like myself, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Did that charged-up period last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate it out day by day. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and could say, okay, this is day one, and then it ended cleanly.\n\nWhat I can say is that the whole scary swing — from after I got fired, when I started feeling really driven and wound up, to where I am now feeling low — has been going on for about a month. The charged-up part was at the beginning of that. I just don’t know exactly how many days to count for that piece by itself.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did it cause serious problems in your life or lead to you being hospitalized?\n\nPatient: I didn’t go to the hospital, no. But serious problems… yeah, I mean, it’s been serious in the sense that I’m not functioning like I normally would.\n\nSince it got bad, I’ve had to lean on my relatives for stuff I used to handle myself. Like money things, getting rides places, dealing with paperwork — forms and calls and things like that. I’ll look at something basic that needs to be done and it just doesn’t get done, or I need somebody to sit with me or take me there or remind me what I’m supposed to do.\n\nAnd errands too. I’m not keeping up with regular errands the way I used to. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m grown and I don’t like needing that much help, but right now I kind of do. So not hospitalized, but it has definitely messed up my life and made me depend on family more than usual.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

But the whole thing, the whole run of it where I haven’t felt like myself, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Did that charged-up period last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate it out day by day. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That was actually one of the things that scared me, because it wasn’t just like normal thinking or being busy in my head. It felt like my head was packed full of ideas all at once, like one thing would pop up and before I even finished thinking about it, it turned into another plan, and then that would turn into something else.

Like I’d think about one job, or getting work, or fixing one problem, and then suddenly I’d be making this whole other plan in my mind, and then another one after that. It just kept going. I’d try to slow it down, like tell myself, “Okay, just focus on one thing,” but I couldn’t really hold it still. My thoughts kept jumping ahead of me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.81, F32.A, F41.9, F50.9, F54\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the biggest thing is I’m not really eating right anymore. Like, I’ll go most of the day and it’s just coffee, maybe a piece of bread if I can make myself grab something. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or anything, it just… food doesn’t really happen. And now my pants are getting loose, like hanging on me, and that kind of scared me when I noticed it. I figured I should probably talk to somebody because it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. Most days it’s like I wake up and before I even really think about anything, there’s this heavy feeling there. Like right in my chest, kind of empty and weighed down at the same time, if that makes sense. It’s not even always that I’m crying or anything dramatic, it’s more like… I open my eyes and it already feels like nothing good is waiting for me. Like the day is just something I have to get through.\n\nI find myself wanting to keep my head down, not really look too far ahead, not think too much. It’s just this low, dark kind of feeling sitting on me most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot, actually. I used to like doing these little things that weren’t anything big, but they felt like mine, you know? Like I’d go walk by the lake, just kind of clear my head and watch the water, take my time out there. I used to really enjoy that.\n\nAnd I had these little model kits I’d work on at home. Nothing fancy, just sitting there with the pieces, putting things together bit by bit. It used to feel calming, like I could get absorbed in it.\n\nNow… they just sit there. The boxes and pieces are still there, and I’ll look at them, but it’s like they belong to somebody else. I don’t feel that pull to pick them up. Even walking by the lake sounds like something I should want to do, but I don’t really feel anything about it. It’s like the enjoyment part got switched off somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I don’t want to say the wrong thing and make it sound bigger or smaller than it is.\n\nI guess… I’ve had moments where I feel really worn out by everything, like I don’t want to deal with the day, but when you say “life isn’t worth living” or “hurting myself,” I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I haven’t really sat down and thought it through like that, if that makes sense. It’s more like this tired, empty feeling, and I just kind of go quiet with it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed myself making any kind of plan or anything like that. I just know I’ve been feeling really low, and it’s been hard to care about much.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food because you’re worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No… no, it’s not really like that. I’m not avoiding food because I’m scared of gaining weight or anything. I don’t think about it that way.\n\nIt’s more like… I just hardly eat right anymore. Food doesn’t really pull me in. Some days I’ll get through most of the day on coffee, and then maybe I’ll have a piece of bread or something small, just because it’s there or because I realize I haven’t had anything. It’s not me trying to lose weight.\n\nI’ve noticed my pants are hanging loose on me now, like I have to keep pulling them up, and that kind of made me realize I’m not eating much. But it’s not about wanting to be thinner. It’s just… eating feels like another thing I don’t really have much drive to do.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a particular stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did kind of start after something big. I got fired, and after that everything sort of… swung really hard. At first it wasn’t just sadness right away, it was like I got this scary kind of driven feeling, like I had to do something, had to prove something, had to keep moving. It felt intense, not normal for me.\n\nAnd then after that, I dropped down into this low place. That whole swing — from getting fired, to feeling all pushed and wound up, and then ending up like this — has been going on about a month now. It feels longer when I’m in it, honestly, but if I count it out, yeah… around a month.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period after you got fired, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I was. That part was really strange, because normally if I only sleep a little, I feel awful the next day. But during that period, I’d sleep maybe two or three hours, sometimes barely that, and then I’d just kind of pop up like I’d had a full night’s rest.\n\nIt wasn’t even like I was dragging myself out of bed. I’d wake up and feel like, “Okay, I’m up, I’ve got things to do,” like my body didn’t understand it was supposed to be tired. I remember looking at the time and thinking it was ridiculous, because it would still be dark out, and I should’ve been exhausted, but I wasn’t. I was just… going.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I mean, “euphoric” sounds a little strong, but I guess there were times it was like that. Right after I got fired, instead of just feeling crushed, I got this feeling like… fine, I’ll show them. Like I was fired up, almost untouchable. Like nobody could really stop me or tell me what to do.\n\nIt wasn’t just confidence like a normal good mood, though. It felt bigger than that, like I was suddenly sure I had everything figured out. And if anyone tried to bring me down or say, “Maybe slow down,” or “Think this through,” I’d get irritated really fast. I’d snap at them before they even finished talking, because in my head they were just getting in the way.\n\nSo yeah, there was that kind of high, charged-up confidence… but it had an angry edge to it too.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I was doing way more than I normally do. It was like I couldn’t sit still at all, like if I sat down for even a minute my body would start buzzing and I’d think, no, I need to get up, I need to do something.\n\nI kept going out and cleaning my tools, even stuff that didn’t really need cleaning. I’d line things up, wipe them down, move them, then come back and mess with them again like there was some important reason for it. And I was moving boxes around too — not even always to a better place, just shifting things, sorting, re-sorting, deciding I had a new plan, then changing it again.\n\nAnd I kept planning jobs in my head, like all these things I was going to do, ways to make money or fix things or get back on track. It felt like I had a motor running inside me. Not just being productive, more like I couldn’t turn it off.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you talking a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was. I didn’t really notice it at first, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining what needed to happen, you know? Like I had all these thoughts lined up and I had to get them out before I lost them.\n\nBut my sister pointed it out. She said I was talking so fast she could barely get a word in. I was going on and on about prices, and making flyers, and what to say to tenants, and how much things should cost, and how I was going to handle everything. I remember her trying to ask me something, and I’d just keep talking over her, not even meaning to be rude exactly, but I couldn’t stop long enough to let her catch up.\n\nIt was like my mouth was trying to keep up with my head, and there was too much coming at once.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, “risky” can mean different things, right? Like, if you mean did I go out and do something crazy dangerous on purpose, I don’t know that I’d say it that way.\n\nDuring that time I was definitely acting faster than I normally would, like making decisions before really thinking them through all the way. But when you ask if it was unusually risky or impulsive, I kind of have to stop and think, because in the moment it didn’t feel risky to me. It felt like I was just doing what needed to be done, like I was finally moving instead of sitting around.\n\nOther people might’ve seen it as me being impulsive, I guess, because I wasn’t exactly pausing and weighing things out. But I don’t have one clear thing in my head where I can say, yes, that was definitely some big risky thing. It was more like everything felt urgent, and I’d just jump into stuff without the usual hesitation.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of hard to pin down exactly to the day, because it didn’t feel like it started and stopped clean, like flipping a switch. But if I’m being honest, that whole scary swing — from when I got fired and then started feeling all driven and wound up, to now being this low — it’s been about a month altogether.\n\nThe charged-up part was in there first, after I got fired, and then it… shifted. But the whole thing, the whole run of it where I haven’t felt like myself, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Did that charged-up period last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate it out day by day. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and could say, okay, this is day one, and then it ended cleanly.\n\nWhat I can say is that the whole scary swing — from after I got fired, when I started feeling really driven and wound up, to where I am now feeling low — has been going on for about a month. The charged-up part was at the beginning of that. I just don’t know exactly how many days to count for that piece by itself.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did it cause serious problems in your life or lead to you being hospitalized?\n\nPatient: I didn’t go to the hospital, no. But serious problems… yeah, I mean, it’s been serious in the sense that I’m not functioning like I normally would.\n\nSince it got bad, I’ve had to lean on my relatives for stuff I used to handle myself. I’m not keeping up with regular errands the way I used to. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m grown and I don’t like needing that much help, but right now I kind of do. So not hospitalized, but it has definitely messed up my life and made me depend on family more than usual.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did your thoughts feel like they were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes. That was actually one of the things that scared me, because it wasn’t just like normal thinking or being busy in my head. It felt like my head was packed full of ideas all at once, like one thing would pop up and before I even finished thinking about it, it turned into another plan, and then that would turn into something else.\n\nLike I’d think about one job, or getting work, or fixing one problem, and then suddenly I’d be making this whole other plan in my mind, and then another one after that. It just kept going. I’d try to slow it down, like tell myself, “Okay, just focus on one thing,” but I couldn’t really hold it still. My thoughts kept jumping ahead of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah, definitely. I mean, way more than normal for me. I wasn’t just thinking, “I should find some work,” I was kind of acting like I had to build this whole thing right then.

It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and could say, okay, this is day one, and then it ended cleanly.\n\nWhat I can say is that the whole scary swing — from after I got fired, when I started feeling really driven and wound up, to where I am now feeling low — has been going on for about a month. The charged-up part was at the beginning of that. I just don’t know exactly how many days to count for that piece by itself.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did it cause serious problems in your life or lead to you being hospitalized?\n\nPatient: I didn’t go to the hospital, no. But serious problems… yeah, I mean, it’s been serious in the sense that I’m not functioning like I normally would.\n\nSince it got bad, I’ve had to lean on my relatives for stuff I used to handle myself. Like money things, getting rides places, dealing with paperwork — forms and calls and things like that. I’ll look at something basic that needs to be done and it just doesn’t get done, or I need somebody to sit with me or take me there or remind me what I’m supposed to do.\n\nAnd errands too. I’m not keeping up with regular errands the way I used to. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m grown and I don’t like needing that much help, but right now I kind of do. So not hospitalized, but it has definitely messed up my life and made me depend on family more than usual.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, did your thoughts feel like they were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes. That was actually one of the things that scared me, because it wasn’t just like normal thinking or being busy in my head. It felt like my head was packed full of ideas all at once, like one thing would pop up and before I even finished thinking about it, it turned into another plan, and then that would turn into something else.\n\nLike I’d think about one job, or getting work, or fixing one problem, and then suddenly I’d be making this whole other plan in my mind, and then another one after that. It just kept going. I’d try to slow it down, like tell myself, “Okay, just focus on one thing,” but I couldn’t really hold it still. My thoughts kept jumping ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: During that charged-up period, were you more goal-directed than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, way more than normal for me. I’d wake up and feel like, “Okay, I’m up, I’ve got things to do,” like my body didn’t understand it was supposed to be tired. I was just… going.\n\nDoctor: During that driven period, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It was a lot more than I’d ever done before.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that charged-up period, did you feel more powerful, special, or capable than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I was telling people I could build my own handyman business easy and show that building they made a big mistake letting me go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I did. At the time it didn’t feel like I was being unrealistic, but looking back, I was talking like I could just build my own handyman business easy, like it was basically already going to work out because I knew I could do it.

I remember telling people, kind of strongly too, that I didn’t need that building, that they made a big mistake letting me go, and I was going to show them. Like, I had this feeling that I was more capable than people had given me credit for, and that once I got started, everybody would see it. I wasn’t just hoping it might work — I was talking like it was obvious, like there was no way I wouldn’t pull it off.

It’s a little embarrassing saying it now, because I was really full of that feeling then. I felt like, “I can do this, I can prove them all wrong,” and I was saying it to people like I had everything figured out already.
